Scope of Testimony 
8 
Q.  What is the purpose of the Staff Gas Pipeline 
9 
Safety Panel’s testimony in this proceeding?  
10 
A. 
The purpose of our testimony is to address 
11 
Central Hudson Gas and Electric Corporation’s, 
12 
also referred to as Central Hudson or the 
13 
Company, proposals regarding safety performance 
14 
measures in the areas of leak management; leak 
15 
prone pipe, or LPP, elimination; emergency 
16 
response; damage prevention; compliance with 
17 
pipeline safety regulations; and associated 
18 
Positive Revenue Adjustment, or PRA, and 
19 
Negative Revenue Adjustment, or NRA, Basis 
20 
Points, or BPs.  The testimony will also address 
21 
Central Hudson’s proposals concerning existing 
22 
and new programs, collectively referred to as 
23 
Gas Safety Programs, and associated PRA BPs.  
24 
2654


--- Page 20 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
    
SPSP 
 
 
8 
 
The existing Gas Safety Programs are as follows: 
1 
Residential Methane Detection, or RMD; Pipeline 
2 
Safety Management System, or PSMS; and Community 
3 
Gas Emergency Response Drill.  The new proposed 
4 
Gas Safety Program is the Leak Prone Services, 
5 
or LPS.  In addition, the Panel will address the 
6 
Company’s proposal to hire eight new full-time 
7 
equivalents, or FTEs, for various departments, 
8 
and the Company’s request for the deferral of 
9 
costs associated with work on Permalock Tapping 
10 
Tees Assemblies.

14

16 
fewer.

2 
Infrastructure Enhancement 
3 
Q. 
What is infrastructure enhancement?  
4 
A. 
Infrastructure enhancement concerns the 
5 
Company’s efforts to remove leak prone pipe.  
6 
Q. 
What is leak prone pipe?  
7 
A. 
Generally, leak prone pipe is unprotected steel 
8 
pipe, cast iron pipe, wrought iron pipe, and 
9 
some vintages of plastic pipe that may become 
10 
brittle.  As the name suggests, pipes made of 
11 
these materials are more prone to gas leakage 
12 
than coated and protected steel and newer 
13 
plastic pipe materials. 
14 
Q. 
What does “unprotected” mean in this context?  
15 
A. 
For purposes of this measure, “unprotected” 
16 
means that the pipe lacks adequate cathodic 
17 
protection, rendering it more susceptible to 
18 
corrosion.  It is also commonly referred to as 
19 
“bare steel” pipe.  Cathodic protection is a 
20 
method by which steel pipelines are protected 
21 
from corrosion by making the surface a cathode, 
22 
thereby inhibiting the formation of iron oxides 
23 
that can result in reduced wall thickness of the 
24 
2663


--- Page 29 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
    
SPSP 
 
 
17 
 
pipe.  Unprotected pipe often has no surface 
1 
coating or inadequate coating, rendering efforts 
2 
to cathodically protect the pipe ineffective or 
3 
uneconomical.   
4 
Q. 
How does the leak prone pipe removal program add 
5 
to the safety of the Company’s gas system?  
6 
A. 
Leaks on underground piping can create safety 
7 
risks to the public and can potentially lead to 
8 
gas-related incidents, such as fires or more 
9 
serious events that could impact health and 
10 
safety, especially under frost conditions.  Leak 
11 
prone pipe generally leaks at a higher rate than 
12 
coated and cathodically protected steel or most 
13 
plastic pipes.  The removal of leak prone pipe 
14 
reduces these safety risks.  
15 
Q. 
Explain the importance of removing unprotected 
16 
or bare steel and cast-iron pipe.  
17 
A. 
Unprotected or bare steel pipes are more prone 
18 
to leakage because they are more susceptible to 
19 
corrosion.  Removal of these pipes reduce risks 
20 
associated with leakage.  As for cast iron pipe, 
21 
it is brittle, susceptible to graphitization, a 
22 
form of corrosion, and has low beam strength.  
23 
Low beam strength means that the material can 
24 
2664


--- Page 30 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
    
SPSP 
 
 
18 
 
fail if it is subjected to heightened loads or a 
1 
loss of ground support, which makes the material 
2 
particularly susceptible to stresses resulting 
3 
from underground disturbances.  These 
4 
disturbances include ground settlement, freeze-
5 
thaw cycles, soil erosion, and nearby excavation 
6 
activities.  Cast iron’s physical 
7 
characteristics therefore make it more prone to 
8 
catastrophic failure than cathodically protected 
9 
steel and most plastic pipe.  Because cast iron 
10 
was installed many decades ago, cast iron pipe 
11 
tends to be in densely populated areas where 
12 
there are many enclosed structures and 
13 
continuous pavement.  In the event of a major 
14 
leak or failure, these circumstances often lead 
15 
to increased migration of below ground gas and 
16 
pose a greater risk of fires or explosions.  
17 
Removal of this type of pipe reduces the 
18 
potential for leaks and incidents resulting from 
19 
its failure.  This will in turn improve the 
20 
public safety.  
21 
Q. 
What are other benefits associated with removing 
22 
LPP?  
23 
A. 
The removal of leak prone pipe should result in 
24 
2665


--- Page 31 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
    
SPSP 
 
 
19 
 
a reduction in the number of active leaks on the 
1 
distribution system, that would ultimately 
2 
reduce overtime and O&M costs associated with 
3 
responding to leak calls, monitoring and 
4 
repairing leaks.  
5 
Q. 
Is there currently a leak prone pipe replacement 
6 
program for Central Hudson?  
7 
A. 
Yes.  Pursuant to the Commission’s 2021 Rate 
8 
Order, Central Hudson is required to replace or 
9 
eliminate 15 miles of LPP per year.   
10 
Q. 
For the past five years, 2018 through 2022, how 
11 
many miles of LPP has the Company replaced or 
12 
eliminated?  
13 
A. 
According to Central Hudson’s response to IR 
14 
DPS-201, the Company replaced or eliminated 
15 
21.4, 21.1, 21.1, 20.1, and 15.1 miles of LPP in 
16 
2018, 2019, 2020, 2021, and 2022, respectively, 
17 
with a five-year average of 19.7 miles.  
18 
Q. 
Are there revenue adjustments associated with 
19 
the elimination of LPP under the Company’s 
20 
current rate plan?  
21 
A. 
Yes.  There is currently an NRA of 15 BPs for 
22 
failure to meet the total mileage target in any 
23 
given year.  There is no positive revenue 
24 
2666


--- Page 32 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
    
SPSP 
 
 
20 
 
adjustment associated with the LPP replacement 
1 
metric.

3

4 
A. 
No.  Central Hudson proposed no changes to its 
5

6 
BPs.

22 
Q.

23 
A. 
We recommend this because the Company must 
24 
2669


--- Page 35 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419

remove leak prone services in conjunction with 
1 
the LPP main removal program.  Leaving bare

Emergency Response 
12 
Q. 
Please describe the emergency response 
13 
performance measure applicable to Central Hudson 
14 
and other LDCs in the state. 
15 
A. 
This measure evaluates the Company’s response 
16 
time to gas leak, odor, and other emergency 
17 
calls generated by the public and by non-company 
18 
personnel.  Each gas LDC is required by 16 NYCRR 
19 
Part 255.825 to provide a monthly report of the 
20 
total number of calls received, along with the 
21 
associated response times broken down into 15-
22 
minute intervals during normal business hours, 
23 
weekdays outside of normal business hours, 
24 
2671


--- Page 37 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
    
SPSP 
 
 
25 
 
weekends, and holidays.   
1 
Q. 
What is the purpose of the emergency response 
2 
performance measure? 
3 
A. 
Leaks on inside piping, improperly operated or 
4 
installed appliances, and gas migrating into a 
5 
building from leaks on outside piping present 
6 
risks to the general public.  The LDCs recognize 
7 
this and dispatch personnel on a priority basis 
8 
in response to calls reporting suspected gas 
9 
leaks or odors.  The LDCs are required to 
10 
maintain a log of such calls and track the 
11 
elapsed time between the dispatching and arrival 
12 
time of qualified service personnel responding 
13 
to the scene.  The longer it takes an LDC to 
14 
respond, the higher the risk and the higher the 
15 
potential of a serious incident or safety threat 
16 
to the general public.  Therefore, it is 
17 
important that LDCs minimize their response 
18 
times for responding to gas leaks and odor 
19 
calls. 
20 
Q. 
Were targets for the emergency response 
21 
performance metric and associated revenue 
22 
adjustments set by the Commission? 
23 
A. 
Yes.  Central Hudson must respond to 75, 90, and 
24 
2672


--- Page 38 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
    
SPSP 
 
 
26 
 
95 percent, of all gas leak and odor calls 
1 
within 30, 45, and 60 minutes, respectively.  
2 
Failure to meet the 75, 90, or 95 percent 
3 
thresholds within 30, 45, or 60 minutes, 
4 
respectively, results in NRAs of 12, 8, and 5 
5 
basis points, respectively.  For its gas leak, 
6 
odor, and emergency calls within the 30 minutes 
7 
response time, Central Hudson has the following 
8 
targets and PRAs: if the Company responds to 
9 
greater than 85, 89, or 92 percent, the Company 
10 
could earn a PRA of 2, 4, or 6 basis points, 
11 
respectively.  
12 
Q. 
How do these targets compare with the standards 
13 
applicable to other LDCs in the state? 
14

Damage Prevention 
17 
Q. 
Please explain damage prevention metric.  
18 
A. 
For this metric, damage prevention refers to the 
19 
Company’s ability to minimize and prevent 
20 
excavation damage to its natural gas system.  
21 
Any damage to a pipeline can result in the 
22 
uncontrolled release of natural gas that could 
23 
potentially lead to an incident.  
24 
2675


--- Page 41 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
    
SPSP 
 
 
29 
 
Q. 
Please describe the performance measures related 
1 
to the prevention of excavation damage.  
2 
A. 
To encourage an LDC to continuously strive to 
3 
improve its performance, targets for damage 
4 
prevention within an LDC’s control have been 
5 
established in rate case proceedings to measure 
6 
the company’s progress in minimizing damages to 
7 
its underground pipeline facilities.  These 
8 
targets included metrics for damages caused by 
9 
mismarks, the company, and its contractors, as 
10 
well as total damages per 1,000 one-call 
11 
tickets.  The total damage category includes 
12 
damages caused by mismarks, the company and its 
13 
contractors, excavator error, as well as damages 
14 
occurring when an excavator did not notify a 
15 
company of its intent to perform excavation 
16 
work.  
17 
Q. 
What is a one-call ticket?  
18 
A. 
The Commission’s pipeline safety regulations 
19 
contained in 16 NYCRR Part 753, Protection of 
20 
Underground Facilities, require excavators to 
21 
make a toll-free call to a one-call notification 
22 
system to provide notice of their intent to 
23 
perform excavation work.  The one-call 
24 
2676


--- Page 42 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
    
SPSP 
 
 
30 
 
notification system is an organization with the 
1 
purpose of establishing and carrying out 
2 
procedures to protect underground facilities 
3 
from damage due to excavation and demolition. 
4 
There are two one-call notification systems in 
5 
New York State: UDig NY, or UDig; and New York 
6 
811, or NY 811.  The one-call notification 
7 
system that covers Central Hudson’s service 
8 
territory is UDig.  UDig collects pertinent 
9 
information from the excavator and transmits it 
10 
to the member utilities that may be affected by 
11 
the excavation work.  Those utilities then mark 
12 
the location of their affected facilities so 
13 
that the excavator can take precautions to avoid 
14 
damaging them.  Each incoming call to UDig will 
15 
generate outgoing notices to the member 
16 
utilities, including gas, electric, telephone, 
17 
cable, water, and sewer companies.  A notice 
18 
received by the utility is referred to as a one-
19 
call ticket.  
20 
Q. 
The Panel indicated that the total damage 
21 
category includes damages caused by mismarks. 
22 
What is a mismark?  
23 
A. 
A mismark occurs when a utility fails to 
24 
2677


--- Page 43 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
    
SPSP 
 
 
31 
 
accurately mark the location of its existing 
1 
underground facilities in response to the one-
2 
call ticket.  Consistent with the requirements 
3 
of 16 NYCRR Part 753, and for the purpose of 
4 
this measure, a mismark is considered any 
5 
instance where the markings are off by more than 
6 
two feet from the location of the underground 
7 
facility.  A mismark also includes any instances 
8 
where the utility fails to mark its facilities 
9 
in response to a properly requested one-call 
10 
ticket.  
11 
Q. 
What are damages by Company and Company 
12 
contractors?  
13 
A. 
These are damages to the gas system facilities 
14 
caused by Company personnel or by contractors 
15 
that are directly working for the Company. 
16 
Q. 
Are there any other categories of damages?  
17 
A. 
Yes.  Third-party excavator error damages are 
18 
historically the largest component of total 
19 
damages, partially because of the effort needed 
20 
to educate third-party contractors in safe and 
21 
best excavation practices.  Most excavators are 
22 
aware of the existence of the one-call system 
23 
and the requirement to notify it of planned 
24 
2678


--- Page 44 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
    
SPSP 
 
 
32 
 
excavation work.  Some excavators are not as 
1 
familiar or experienced in the additional 
2 
requirements such as tolerance zones and 
3 
verifying locations of underground facilities 
4 
with hand-dug test holes, maintaining the marks 
5 
throughout the full work period, maintaining 
6 
clearances when using powered equipment, etc. 
7 
There is not a target specifically for third-
8 
party excavator damages.  However, third-party 
9 
excavator damage is a major component of the 
10 
total damage category; thus, the Company should 
11 
seek to minimize these damages.  
12 
Q. 
Please explain how prevention of excavation 
13 
damage benefits and protects public safety.  
14 
A. 
Reducing these types of damages improves public 
15 
safety by minimizing uncontrolled gas releases 
16 
that can cause explosions, fires, injuries, and 
17 
fatalities.  These damages to gas facilities 
18 
often cause interruptions of service to 
19 
customers, building evacuations, and road 
20 
closures.

Q.

24 
2688

Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
    
SPSP 
 
 
42 
 
proposed changes? 
1 
A. 
No.  Pursuant to the 2021 Rate Order, for rate 
2 
case established targets, the Total Damage Rate 
3 
includes the hand excavation damages with a one- 
4 
call ticket.  However, hand excavation damages 
5 
without a one call ticket are excluded from the 
6

7

8

9 
because excavators digging solely with hand or 
10 
animal power are not required to provide notice 
11 
of intent to excavate.  Accordingly, the Company 
12 
is not afforded an opportunity to mark its 
13 
facilities and typically bears no responsibility 
14 
for these damages.  In cases with a valid one 
15 
call ticket, however, the Company was afforded 
16 
an opportunity to mark its facilities.  But 
17 
under the Company’s proposal, a facility damaged 
18 
by hand tools would necessarily be excluded from 
19 
the metric even though the root cause of the 
20 
damage may have been within the Company’s 
21 
control, such as the Company not marking, 
22 
mismarking, or inaccurately marking its facility 
23 
in response to the excavator’s notice of intent 
24 
2689


--- Page 55 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
    
SPSP 
 
 
43 
 
to excavate.  Additionally, even though hand 
1 
excavation to identify gas facilities is 
2 
sometimes required under 16 NYCRR Part 753, 
3 
excavators nevertheless have a duty to exercise 
4 
caution while excavating around underground 
5 
facilities.  This duty extends to the use of 
6 
hand tools.  For all of these reasons, damages 
7 
due to hand digging should not be totally 
8 
excluded from the Total Damage Rate.

Compliance With the Pipeline Safety Regulations 
10 
Q.

12 
A. 
Yes.  All LDCs in New York State must comply

with the Commission’s pipeline safety rules and 
14 
regulations contained in 16 NYCRR Parts 255, 
15 
261, and 753.  
16 
Q. 
Panel, is there any distinction between the 
17 
terms “violation” and “occurrence?” 
18 
A. 
Pipeline Safety Staff uses the term occurrence 
19 
to describe the number of instances that a 
20 
specific violation takes place, but for purposes 
21 
of this compliance measure, there is no 
22 
difference between the terms “violation” and 
23 
“occurrence.”  Historically, negative revenue 
24 
2690


--- Page 56 ---
Cases 23-E-0418 & 23-G-0419 
    
SPSP 
 
 
44 
 
adjustment letters noted the violation of a 
1 
specific requirement and identified the total 
2 
number of occasions that requirement was 
3 
violated.  The term violation is commonly 
4 
referred to in discussions and is widely 
5 
understood within the pipeline industry.  These 
6 
words are and can be used interchangeably.  
7 
Q. 
How are violations of these regulations 
8 
identified and investigated?  
9 
A.  The Department’s Pipeline Safety Section Staff 
10 
conducts annual record and field audits of 
11 
Central Hudson, investigates incidents involving 
12 
the Company’s natural gas facilities, and 
13 
investigates complaints from the public related 
14 
to issues such as gas odors.  Typically, when 
15 
Staff discovers an instance of non-compliance 
16 
with the Commission’s pipeline safety 
17 
regulations, a compliance meeting is held with 
18 
the Company to detail all instances of probable 
19 
non-compliance with the gas safety regulation 
20 
sections.  
21 
Q. 
What is the purpose of the compliance meeting? 
22 
A. 
The compliance meeting is an opportunity for the 
23 
Company to provide information clarifying the 
24

VII. 
CLCPA AND CAC SCOPING PLAN 
10 
Q.    
What is the CLCPA?  
11 
A. 
The CLCPA is New York State legislation that was signed into law on July 
12 
18, 2019.  The CLCPA mandates substantial reductions in GHG 
13 
emissions across all sectors of the state’s economy and accelerated 
14 
deployment of clean energy technologies.  There are many critical 
15 
milestones established within the CLCPA, including a 40% reduction in 
16 
statewide GHG emissions by 2030, 70% of electricity generated by 
17 
renewable sources by 2030, 100% of electricity generated by emissions 
18 
free sources by 2040, and an 85% reduction in statewide GHG emissions 
19 
by 2050.  The CLCPA also has provisions to ensure benefits of clean 
20 
energy investments accrue to disadvantaged communities.   
21 
2787


--- Page 153 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE CLIMATE LEADERSHIP AND 
SUSTAINABILITY PANEL 
9 
 
Q.    
What is the Scoping Plan? 
1 
A. 
The CLCPA directed the establishment of a Climate Action Council 
2 
(“CAC”), which is a 22-member appointed body charged with developing a 
3 
Scoping Plan to serve as the roadmap to achieve the State’s progressive 
4 
clean energy and climate goals.  In accordance with the CLCPA, the CAC 
5 
developed its final Scoping Plan in December of 2022, after releasing its 
6 
Draft Scoping Plan in 2021 and a subsequent period of public review and 
7 
input.  The Scoping Plan presents three Scenarios which serve as 
8 
proposed roadmaps for how the State might achieve the goals of the 
9 
CLCPA, and recommendations for additional legislation and new 
10 
regulations affecting the electricity and gas sectors plus the transportation, 
11 
buildings, industry, agriculture, forestry, and waste sectors.  Estimated 
12 
costs through 2050 of these recommendations, as calculated from the 
13 
Plan’s technical appendices, are about $300 billion in 2020 in net present 
14 
value terms.  To the extent that these recommendations are adopted, 
15 
Central Hudson along with the other utilities in New York and their 
16 
customers will face rapidly increasing requirements to support new 
17 
renewable generation and electrification initiatives in the transportation 
18 
and buildings sectors.   
19 
Q.    
Why is Central Hudson seeking a deferral mechanism for costs related to 
20 
CLCPA compliance?  
21 
2788


--- Page 154 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE CLIMATE LEADERSHIP AND 
SUSTAINABILITY PANEL 
10 
 
A. 
To meet the near- and mid-term milestones set forth in the CLCPA, it is 
1 
likely that new legislation and/or regulations will require accelerated 
2 
development of new programs, technologies, projects, and other 
3 
compliance efforts beyond what is proposed by this Panel, the costs of 
4 
which are unknown and uncertain at this time.  To the extent that CLCPA 
5 
compliance requires the Company to make incremental investments within 
6 
the term of this rate plan, and those costs are not otherwise addressed 
7 
within generic proceedings, the deferral would ensure the necessary 
8 
funding is available to fully comply with state requirements on a timely 
9 
basis.  This deferral has been reflected within the testimony of the 
10 
Accounting and Tax Panel.

9

which should include and embrace a wide variety of technologies,

13 
dedicated to meeting the expectations and requirements established by its 
14 
stakeholders through the integration and implementation of sustainability 
15 
principles (that cover areas from environmental protection to just 
16 
transition) and climate change strategies.  Central Hudson’s strategy and 
17 
business planning processes reflect this approach.  We are collaborating 
18 
with peer companies, industry organizations and others to explore 
19 
innovative solutions that meet our modernization and sustainability 
20 
objectives.  Central Hudson has increased engagement and strives to be

1 
C. GAS INITIATIVES 
2 
Q.    
What solutions has Central Hudson implemented to reduce natural gas-
3 
related emissions? 
4 
A. 
Central Hudson has implemented several initiatives including Operational 
5 
Methane Reduction, the Enhanced Leak-Prone Pipe (“LPP”) Elimination 
6 
Program, NPAs, and Responsibly Sourced Gas (“RSG”).   
7 
Q.    
Please describe the Company’s Operational Methane Reduction efforts.   
8 
A. 
The Company has begun piloting natural gas recompression technologies 
9 
on Gas Transmission projects.  Since 2020, this has resulted in the direct 
10 
capture of 114 thousand standard cubic feet (“MSCF”) of methane 
11 
emissions during transmission purging operations that would have 
12 
normally been vented directly to the atmosphere.  The Company has been 
13 
proactive in addressing fugitive emissions from its distribution operations 
14 
(setting repair goals that exceed the State’s mandated timeframes) and 
15 
has been successful in substantially reducing its annual leak backlog over 
16 
the past decade.  The Company continues to replace aging equipment at 
17 
regulator stations with equipment that reduces or eliminates the direct 
18 
release of natural gas into the atmosphere.  Finally, the Company 
19 
regularly supports innovative solutions and R&D projects that promote 
20 
detecting leaks sooner from both a safety and GHG emissions 
21 
perspective. 
22 
2797


--- Page 163 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE CLIMATE LEADERSHIP AND 
SUSTAINABILITY PANEL 
19 
 
Q.    
What are the environmental benefits from the Company’s operational 
1 
methane reduction efforts? 
2 
Q.    
Central Hudson’s operational methane reduction efforts increase the 
3 
efficiencies of the delivery of natural gas to its customers.  Delivering 
4 
natural gas more efficiently reduces the amount of direct GHG emissions 
5 
being released into the environment while supporting the climate change 
6 
goals established by the CLCPA.   
7 
Q.    
What environmental benefits has Central Hudson achieved through its 
8 
Enhanced LPP Elimination Program? 
9 
A. 
Central Hudson began its Enhanced LPP Elimination Program in 2016.  
10 
Since the start of the program through 2022, the Company has reduced 
11 
the fugitive methane emissions associated with LPP by approximately 
12 
66%.  More information on this initiative can be found in the direct 
13 
testimony of the Gas Capital and Operations and Gas Safety Panels.   
14 
Q.    
Please summarize Central Hudson’s NPA program. 
15 
A. 
The NPA program consists of projects designed to displace the need for a 
16 
traditional gas infrastructure investment.  In the context of avoiding capital 
17 
for LPP elimination, an NPA project seeks to convert natural gas 
18 
customers to customers who are supplied by alternate forms of energy, 
19 
such as electricity, allowing for the retirement of non-essential sections of 
20 
LPP.  In its Initial Implementation Plan and Compliance filing on June 14, 
21 
2019, within Cases 17-E-0459 and 17-G-0460, Central Hudson entitled 
22 
2798


--- Page 164 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE CLIMATE LEADERSHIP AND 
SUSTAINABILITY PANEL 
20 
 
this type of NPA a “Transportation Mode Alternative.” As of March 31, 
1 
2023, Central Hudson has completed four Transportation Mode 
2 
Alternative projects in Newburgh, Beacon, Fishkill, and Poughkeepsie.  
3 
Through these efforts, nine homes have been fully electrified, and 2,072 
4 
feet of LPP has successfully been retired.  Additional locations are 
5 
currently under review or in various stages of marketing and 
6

Q.    
What is RSG? 
19 
A. 
RSG is natural gas obtained from suppliers that proactively manage their 
20 
methane emissions through an independent third-party measurement and 
21 
certification to attest that the gas was produced under specified best 
22 
2800


--- Page 166 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE CLIMATE LEADERSHIP AND 
SUSTAINABILITY PANEL 
22 
 
practices for methane mitigation as well as best practices for other vital 
1 
environmental categories, such as water use, land use or community 
2 
engagement. 
3 
Q.    
What are the benefits of RSG? 
4 
A. 
The Company has determined through a recent pilot project that the 
5 
procurement and distribution of RSG has a significant impact on reducing 
6 
GHG emissions compared to traditionally sourced natural gas.  This 
7 
indicates that utilities can successfully influence natural gas production 
8 
companies to invest in technologies and equipment to certify their natural 
9 
gas product as responsibly sourced.  RSG results in an approximately 
10 
82% reduction in upstream production (wellhead) emissions which is the 
11 
primary source of natural gas emissions.  RSG can also be delivered at a 
12 
minimal incremental cost compared to other emissions reduction 
13 
alternatives (e.g., Renewable Natural Gas).   
14 
Q.    
Describe the Company’s current RSG efforts. 
15 
A. 
In support of the CLCPA’s decarbonization efforts, Central Hudson has 
16 
prioritized the purchase of RSG over traditionally sourced gas when 
17 
possible.  As detailed in the Electric and Gas Procurement Panel 
18 
testimony, Central Hudson is currently required to seek the lowest 
19 
reasonable natural gas and electric supply purchase costs for its 
20 
customers, subject to reliability and contractual constraints.  The Company 
21 
will continue to request separate pricing for RSG as part of its periodic 
22

1 
pricing is at or less than the weighted average cost of natural gas supply.

2802


--- Page 168 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE CLIMATE LEADERSHIP AND 
SUSTAINABILITY PANEL 
24 
 
Q.    
How does a third-party firm certify that the RSG is produced under the 
1 
best practices for methane mitigation and best practices? 
2 
A. 
The GHG emissions reductions are verified through the measurement and 
3 
reporting of operational and fugitive methane emissions at upstream 
4 
natural gas facilities.  Continuous (24/7, minute-by-minute), emissions 
5 
monitoring devices are utilized to detect, locate and quantify leaks, 
6 
allowing operators to make quick decisions to mitigate the effects and 
7 
accurately measure methane intensity.  The Environmental Defense Fund 
8 
has recognized the value of continuous monitoring in its Certification of 
9 
Natural Gas with Low Methane Emissions: Criteria for Credible 
10 
Certification Programs3 white paper.  
11 
Q.    
How does the procurement of RSG align with the State’s emission 
12 
reduction goals? 
13 
A. 
By comparing the methane intensity of the RSG with the average methane 
14 
intensity of the traditionally extracted natural gas, the Company can 
15 
calculate the CO2e abated.  Under Environmental Conservation Law 75-
16 
0101(13), statewide GHG emissions include upstream out-of-state GHG 
17 
emissions associated with the generation of electricity imported into the 
18 
State, or the extraction, transmission, and use of fossil fuels imported into 
19 
the State. 
20 
 
3  Lackner, Maureen, Mohlin, Kristina, Certification of Natural Gas with Low Methane 
Emissions: Criteria for Credible Certification Programs,” EDF_Certification_White-Paper.pdf. 
(Accessed April 1, 2023) 
2803

1 
C. ELECTRIFICATION OF FLEET 
2 
Q.    
Why is Central Hudson proposing to electrify a significant portion of its 
3 
vehicle fleet? 
4 
A. 
Vehicle GHG emissions are the second largest direct GHG emissions 
5 
from Central Hudson’s operations.  The replacement of gasoline and 
6 
diesel vehicles with electric vehicles reduces GHG emissions and 
7 
minimizes environmental impacts.  EVs do not utilize motor oils to operate 
8 
their engines, which eliminates the potential for an unplanned release into 
9 
the environment during operations throughout the service territory.  
10 
Central Hudson’s fleet is also highly visible to our customers.  Our utility 
11 
operations require over 811 vehicles which are driven approximately 5.6 
12 
million miles per year.  Through electrification of its fleet, Central Hudson’s 
13 
will lead by example, further encouraging customers to consider electric 
14 
alternatives for their personal vehicles commercial vehicles, or fleets.     
15 
Q.    
Does Central Hudson have additional fleet electrification goals? 
16 
A. 
Central Hudson has committed to spending at least 10% of our annual 
17 
vehicle capital budget on the procurement of battery electric vehicle and/or 
18 
plug in hybrid electric vehicles through 2025 and 50% of the annual 
19 
vehicle capital budget or more by 2030 to infrastructure and electric-heavy 
20 
duty vehicles and equipment.  Additional details on the Company’s fleet 
21 
2810


--- Page 176 ---
Case 23-E-____; Case 23-G-____ 
 
DIRECT TESTIMONY OF THE CLIMATE LEADERSHIP AND 
SUSTAINABILITY PANEL 
32 
 
electrification goal can be found in the direct testimony of the Common 
1 
Capital and Operations Panel.

CLEAN ENERGY INVESTMENTS  
16 
Q.    
Did Dutchess County comment generally on the clean energy investments 
17 
proposed by the Company? 
18 
A. 
Yes.  Dutchess County expressed its concern about the overall cost of 
19 
achieving state clean energy policy objectives and the impact such costs 
20 
will have on customers.  Dutchess County states that emissions reduction 
21 
efforts within New York State will have little impact on the global climate 
22 
2823


--- Page 189 ---
Case 23-E-0418; Case 23-G-0419

4 
 
and that New Yorkers, including those residing in disadvantaged 
1

2

What is the Panel’s response to Dutchess County’s concerns?  
5

The Panel acknowledges Dutchess County’s concerns regarding the 
6

7 
energy goals.  The future costs, as well as responsibility for those costs, 
8 
are not fully understood at this time.  The Company supports a balance, 
9 
one where safety, reliability, and just and reasonable rates are core 
10 
elements of the Company’s utility planning and operations, while the 
11 
incorporation of clean energy initiatives provides support for the 
12 
achievement of New York’s CLCPA targets.  As Dutchess County 
13 
indicates in its testimony, “Through PSC regulation and orders, balance is 
14 
defined.”  The Company’s clean energy investments are consistent with 
15 
those included in recent Public Service Commission (“PSC” or 
16 
“Commission”) orders approving utility rate plans.    
17

Does Central Hudson agree it should not move forward with its proposed 
18 
initiatives? 
19

20 
a modest cost to customers and should not be rejected simply because 
21 
they are not currently mandated.  As described in the Panel’s direct 
22

statewide progress in emissions reductions from 2019 levels toward the

IV. 
CONSISTENCY WITH CLCPA   
15 
Q.  
What are the requirements of CLCPA Sections 7(2) and 7(3)?  
16 
A. 
Section 7(2) requires the Commission to determine whether the 
17 
Company’s rate filing is consistent with the CLCPA’s emissions reduction 
18 
goals.  Section 7(3) requires the Commission to determine that the 
19 
Company’s rate filings do not disproportionately burden DACs. 
20 
Q. 
Did Staff’s testimony address Central Hudson’s rate proposal in the 
21 
context of CLCPA Sections 7(2) and 7(3)?

3 
7(3).  The SPP generally indicates that the Company’s filing, with the

1

2

3

4

5

6

8

9 
demonstrate urgency towards the 2050 goal; and (3) seeks to invest in an 
10 
unvetted technology that has known problems and can exacerbate 
11 
inequity.  Further, AD103 raises concerns related to DACs and NOX 
12 
emissions.   
13 
Q.  
Does the Hydrogen Study support both the goals and spirit of the CLCPA? 
14 
A.  
Yes, the Hydrogen Study is consistent with the CLCPA.  The CLCPA does 
15 
not mandate which combination of technologies will be used to achieve 
16 
the greenhouse gas emissions reduction goals, and hydrogen presents a 
17 
significant opportunity for emissions reduction which may contribute 
18 
towards CLCPA targets.  The Hydrogen Study is a first step to evaluate a 
19 
potential hydrogen pathway for sectors of the economy where 
20 
electrification is difficult or infeasible.  Conducting the Hydrogen Study 
21 
does not represent a tradeoff between reducing greenhouse gas 
22

emissions at an industrial site at the cost of 2030 and 2040 zero emission

equity goals.  As described in the Panel’s direct testimony, the Company

Q.    
Does the Company have a recent example of a regulatory requirement 
1 
related to the CLCPA for which there was no provision for cost recovery? 
2 
A. 
Yes, pursuant to the Commission’s Order Adopting Gas System Planning 
3 
Process, (“Gas Planning Order”) issued May 12, 2022 in Case 20-G-0131, 
4 
Central Hudson is required to develop and file a Long-Term Plan in 
5

6

7 
efforts, requiring the support of two experienced consultants.  Through 
8 
November 2023, the Company has incurred costs that had not previously 
9 
been forecasted in development of this plan and expects to incur more 
10 
costs in the subsequent months.  Importantly, the Gas Planning Order did 
11 
not explicitly allow Central Hudson to recover the costs associated with 
12 
this study or defer the costs for future recovery.  
13 
Q.    
Can Central Hudson defer and/or petition for cost recovery for this type of 
14 
expense?  
15 
A. 
In some instances, yes.  But when determining if deferral accounting 
16 
treatment is appropriate, the Commission generally follows the three-
17 
prong test: 1) the expense must be incremental to the amount allowed in 
18 
rates; 2) the incremental amount must be material (i.e., 5% of net income 
19 
attributable to shareholders before the event occurred) and extraordinary 
20 
in nature; and 3) earnings must be below the authorized rate of return on 
21 
common equity.  In the case of the Long-Term Plan, conditions 1) and 3) 
22 
2847


--- Page 213 ---

REBUTTAL TESTIMONY OF THE CLIMATE LEADERSHIP AND 
SUSTAINABILITY PANEL 
28 
 
would be satisfied, but costs did not meet the materiality threshold of 5% 
1 
net income within a particular rate year.  Despite this, the costs are still a 
2 
necessary expense for the Company to incur without the ability to recover.
